后記

本报告系由卢兆荫、李德金、姚苑慎、冯普仁四同志集体编写，由卢兆荫同志主编。稿子经过考古研究所所长夏鼐同志审阅。在编写过程中，考古研究所王伯洪、王仲殊二同志曾提供许多有益的意见。墓志考释承考古研究所陈梦家先生提出修改意见。

绘图和照像工作皆由考古研究所技术室担任。插图绝大多数是曹国鉴和冼自强同志绘制的，器物照片为赵、刘国强、韩悦三位同志所摄。英文提要是考古研究所王俊铭同志翻译的。

中国科学院考古研究所西安研究室

1965年10月31日

The Sui and T'ang Tombs on the Outskirts of Sian

This report deals with the 175 Sui and T'ang tombs excavated in 1955-1961 on the eastern and western outskirts of Sian. Of the 136 tombs excavated on the eastern outskirts, 5 were located at Hung-ch'ing Village, 22 at Kub-chia-t'an and 109 at Hanshen-chai. Of the 39 tombs excavated on the western outskirts, 16 were located at K'esheng-chuang and 23 at Chang-chia-p'o (Fig. 1).

A study of other excavated tombs of this period and a systematic classification and careful study of the large amount of objects unearthed from these tombs have enabled the author to trace the development of the tomb structure and the changes which took place in regard to tomb furniture. Using the changes which took place in regard to the main tomb figures as the chief criterion but without ignoring the value of the bronze mirror, coins and tomb plan as corroborative evidence, he has divided these 175 tombs into three choronological periods.

In accordance with their structures, the tombs are divided into four main types: Type I, II, III and IV. Tombs of Type I all belong to the first period. Type IIa appears in all three periods while Types IIb and IIc are found only in the second period. Type IIIa, IIIb and IIIc can also be found in all three periods but Type IIId appears only in the third period. Tombs of Type III, the most numerous of the four main types, are the commonest type in the Tang Dynasty. Tombs of Type IV are not further divided due to scarcity of tomb furniture.

The following is a description of the main characteristics of each type:

Tombs of Type I have a tomb chamber which is wider from east to west and narrower from north to south. The tomb passage is located at the centre of the south wall of the chamber or slightly to the west. Tombs of this type can be further divided into three sub-types: Ia, Ib and Ic (Figs. 2-4 & 6). Tombs of Type II have a square or oblong chamber with the tomb passage located at the centre of its south wall or slightly to the east. The plan of the whole tomb somewhat resembles a spade in shape. Tombs of this type can likewise be divided into three sub-types: IIa, IIb and IIc (Figs. 5 & 7-9). A tomb of Type IIc (Tomb No. 305) has a brick double-room chamber made up by the tomb passage, front-chamber passage, front chamber, rear-chamber passage and rear chamber. The southern end of the front-chamber passage is sealed with bricks but slightly to the north is a stone gate with carved decorations (Figs. 9-14). Tombs of Type III usually have an oblong chamber but in a few cases the chamber is square in shape. The tomb passage is located on the east side of its south wall and in some cases the east wall of the passage links up with the east wall of the chamber either in a straight line or at an angle. In a few cases, the east wall of the passage bulges out from the east wall of the chamber. The plan of the whole tomb resembles a handled knife with the chamber

constituting the blade and the passage the handle. Tombs of this type can be further divided into four sub-types: IIIa, IIIb, IIIc and IIIId (Figs. 15-25). Tombs of Type IV, invariably small tombs, usually have a narrow oblong chamber which is practically of the same width as the tomb passage (Figure 26).

Sixty-five out of the 175 tombs of this group have yielded a total of 775 pottery tomb figures representing tomb-guardians, men, women and animals. Among the tomb-guardians are monsters (Plates IV—VII, 1—2), warriors (Plates VII, 3; VIII, 1; IX, 1 & XI, 1), lokapalas (Plates VIII, 2—4; IX, 2 & X) and symbolic animals of the twelve double-hours (Plate VII, 4—5). The male figures can be divided into eight groups according to the type of headaddress each of them wears (Fig. 27; Plates XI, 2—5; XII—XIX, 1—4) while the female figures are divided into six groups according to their hair styles (Fig. 28; Plates XIX, 5—9; XX—XXIII, 5—6). The animals include the chicken, dog, pig, sheep (goat), horse and cattle (Plates XXIII, 1—4, 7—8; XXIV—XXVII, 1—3).

An overwhelming majority of the pottery figures are mould-made, with only a few small animal figures which are handmade. A few of the male figures and animals were made in single-valve moulds but the majority were made in bi-valve moulds. The valves of the moulds for human figures were so arranged as to form the front and rear halves of the body respectively while those of the moulds for animals joined each other from left and right. Usually, the figures were first covered with a white slip and then painted with coloured decorations. The faces of the human figures are often painted in pink while the lips are painted in vermilion. The eyebrows, eyes, beards, hair as well as the headdresses and boots are all drawn in black ink. The garments are painted in such colours as the red, yellow, green, blue and brown. Some of the large tomb-guardians are further painted with gold. The pair of lokapalas unearthed from Tomb No. 203 with their vigorous expressions and brilliant ornaments can rival the best of the Tang Dynasty clay figures of lokapalas found in the cave temples of Tunghuang (Plate X, 1-2).

Apart from the pottery tomb figures, the tomb furniture includes pottery vessels, tri-coloured glazed pottery vessels, glazed pottery, porcelains, bronzes, vessels and objects of iron and lead, objects or vessels of jade, stone, bone, shell, glass and lacquer, as well as coins and epitaph stones.

The great variety of ceramics unearthed is among the more important finds from this group of tombs and includes such types as the green glazed ware, various types of celadon, brown glazed ware, black glazed ware, white ware and a mixed clay ware. Some of the porcelains are of a rather high quality in regard to both paste and glaze colour. For instance, the spitton unearthed from the tomb of Lu Wu (Tomb No. 586), which was built in the twelfth year of the K'ai Huang reign period (A.D. 592), is characterized by a fine pure white paste and a brilliant white glaze (Plate XXXV, 2). The lotus leaf shaped teacup-stand unearthed from the tomb of Lu Fu-yuan (Tomb No. 420), which was built in the twelfth year of the Ta Chung reign period (A.D. 858), is distinguished by the beauty of form and a very thin paste covered by a translucent white glaze tinged

with a touch of blue (Plate XXXVII, 5-6). A celadon box-cover unearthed from Tomb No. 527 is decorated in relief with a butterfly surrounded by some brown patches (Plate XXXVII, 3). This type of new ware is an advance over the monochrome celadons in regard to decoration. The discovery of these porcelains has provided some valuable data for the study of the development of Chinese porcelains.

Sixteen out of the 175 tombs of the group have yielded a total of 18 epitaphs. Except for one which is of brick the rest are all of stone and most of them are carved with decorations. The dates they bear are of great help to the dating of this group of tombs and their tomb furniture. The texts of these epitaphs are published along with some notes in the appendix.

The tomb furniture found in the tombs of each period is marked by some distinctive characteristics. For instance, the modelling of the pottery tomb figures found in the tombs of the first period is rather stiff and shows the artistic influence of the Northern Dynasties. Judging by the dates borne by some of the epitaphs, the tombs of this period probably date from the Sui Dynasty or the early Tang (late sixth century to late seventh century). In the second period, some of the tomb figures and mortuary objects are largely copied from those of the first period in spite of some stylistic difference, but others, such as the lokapalas, symbolic animals of the twelve double-hours, foreigners with p'u t'ou headdress, officials with tall headdress (Plate XI, 2; XVII, 5), women with various types of hair styles (Fig. 28; Plate XXII) and two kinds of lobed bronze mirrors (Plates XLIV, 1-3; XLV, 1-2), are all new types and stylistically much more diversified than those of the first period. The tomb figures are all very lifelike and richly ornamented. Particularly striking is the ferociousness of the tomb guardians while the dignity and plumpness of the female figures are so characteristic of the High Tang style and offer a sharp contrast to the slim and delicate figures of the previous period. Judging by the dates borne by some of the epitaphs, the tombs of this period belong to the High Tang (late seventh century to middle eighth century). In the third period, most of the tomb furniture are similar to those of the second period but artistically of an inferior quality. There are also some differences in regard to style and quantity. In contrast to those of the High Tang, the tomb figures tend to be smaller and more delicate and elaborate while the female figures also suffer from an excessive plumpness. The dates borne by some of the epitaphs suggest that the tombs of this period date from the late Tang (middle eighth century to early tenth century).

The excavation of such a large number of Sui and Tang tombs has yielded a great variety of tomb furniture, including some of considerable artistic and historical value. The publication of these finds will provide some reliable data for the study of the productive forces, social life and ideology of this period.
